ParlaMint-AT_2006-03-29-022-XXII-NRSITZ-00142_d7e25501	Mr President. Mr. State Secretary! High house! First of all, I would like to thank you very much for the approval by all four political groups of this House of this new kind of reporting and for the fact that the Court of Auditors has carried out parallel audits with the Court of Auditors of other countries, in this case with Swiss financial control. This is therefore important for the Court of Auditors, because, as you know, in times of globalisation and increased international cooperation within the framework of intergovernmental treaties, the tasks of the Courts of Auditors are becoming more and more similar, and it is also true that, in increasing cooperation within the framework of the EU, cooperation between national accounting authorities is necessary, and is absolutely essential. You know, ladies and gentlemen, that, for example, in the area of cross-border transport projects, in environmental conservation projects, in areas where procurement and development projects are carried out between several countries, as well as in areas where there is actually a loss of revenue such as VAT and VAT fraud. The Austrian Court of Auditors has put forward these tasks, as you have said positively here, namely, that it has also given the EU Presidency of Austria the opportunity to develop a position paper, in the form that the Court of Auditors is interested in the fact that EU funds are also used sparingly, economically and expediently. In the last ten years, the European Court of Auditors has not been able to give full confirmation, i.e. due to the shortcomings, due to errors in the control system, a proper testat was not possible. In this case, as I believe, it is also a task of the Austrian Court of Auditors to set up initiatives on this subject by the EU net payer Austria. At any rate, I am pleased that the initiative towards positions, how to actually improve the control system in the EU, has been positively welcomed, that this has come about in large reluctance both in Austria and in the EU as a whole. On the other hand, this parallel audit was carried out with the Federal Financial Control, with a view – which has already been mentioned – to a systematic comparison of external audits, in particular with regard to VAT, in order to gain experience and key figures and learn from them within the framework of the Best Practice. Another important point, because you have already dealt with an external audit of the financial offices here, in the first phase. This was with the audit report 7/2005, which showed how strong or good the external audit of the financial administration in Austria is. The same thing was done in Switzerland. These two tests are involved in this examination, which is now under treatment to show exactly where there are strengths, where there are weaknesses – and especially where you can learn from each other. Even if the time is already a little advanced, I allow myself to say here: I think it is appropriate to point out where there are significant differences and in which areas the Austrian or the Swiss financial administration can learn from each other. It was also addressed that entry for tax liability in Switzerland is more than twice as high as in Austria. In Switzerland there is an entry limit for VAT from €48 585, in Austria it is €22 000. This leads to a total of 312,000 businesses registered in Switzerland and 1.1 million in Austria. That is to say, in Switzerland there are 60 percent small businesses, in Austria 92 percent. If you compare this with regard to large businesses, you can see that Austria is a little behind it: in Switzerland 38 000 large businesses, in Austria 18 500. One important aspect is also the reform of financial management, as well as: How does the survey take place, how does control take place in Switzerland and Austria? It is true that in Switzerland the external audit, especially in the case of VAT, is carried out centrally, but the collection of taxes on income and wealth or income is carried out by the 26 cantons, that is, decentralised. In Austria, we have a central survey of both sales tax and income and income tax. In Austria we have 1 500 testers, in Switzerland, as regards VAT, 160 testers. It is also true that control density in Austria is much stronger. In Austria we have a total of 42 500 external examinations, in Switzerland 6 700. Another comparison with regard to examination intervals: in Switzerland, for large businesses, an examination interval of 19 years, in Austria of three years. As regards the examination intervals of medium-sized enterprises: in Austria 19 years, in Switzerland 37 years. As regards the case selection – also an important aspect – it is so that in Switzerland the auditors can decide which cases are actually under examination. That is, the examiner gets a list of 200 cases printed out, and he works them off. In Austria – and I think the Commissioner Puswald is also – there is a time, group and case selection and the examiner has no way to influence the test result. [[Abg. May. Molterer: Does he know?]] [[Abg. May. Molterer: Whether the Puswald knows?]] It was mentioned that it would be appropriate and appropriate to compare this result in the framework of the Best Practice to raise the VAT limit on small businesses. It has been proposed, at €35 000, and it is also gratifying that the financial management in this respect has taken up this intention and will continue to pursue. In addition – and now we have the importance to carry out both audits – it has been shown that sales tax pre-registrations in Austria are only to be made from an annual turnover limit of € 100 000. In other words, it would also be appropriate to be able to make a risk-oriented choice and, as in Switzerland, to relieve small businesses, to raise the VAT limit to €35 000 in this area on the one hand, but at the same time to ensure that VAT applications are issued earlier, or less than €100 000, in order to be more efficient and target-oriented. I refer to the fact that only the external audit of the financial offices has shown that two out of three sales tax audits amounted to less than €3,600; that is, one has chosen cases that were not very efficient. This is probably because the data was not available to carry out a risk-oriented selection. Finally, I would like to point out that the Court's staff have been thanked. I would also like to give this thanks and also thank the examiners. In particular, I would also like to thank my colleagues from Switzerland – and I hope you will – who, through his constructive cooperation, has certainly also contributed significantly to the fact that the experience of this parallel audit can be considered extremely positive. Thank you.
